My clients, the housing market, my family, money… even silly things like whether moving to the condo was the right decision, and I know lots of people downsize, and it’s probably fine, but I keep turning it over in my head. It just feels bigger than it should, compared to what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’m avoiding things exactly… I mean, I still go to work, I still deal with clients and showings and all that. Sometimes I’ll put off making a call because I’m overthinking how it’ll go, but then in real estate there are always calls you don’t want to make, you know? It’s hard to tell if that’s worry or just… being tired of people changing their minds every five minutes. So, no, I can’t think of a big situation I’ve stopped doing because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or difficulty sleeping?\n\nPatient: Mostly sleep, I think. I do get to bed at a normal time—well, I try to, anyway—but then my mind just starts running. Condo fees, what showings I have the next day, whether I missed some tiny thing in a contract… and then I’m thinking about how one missed detail can turn into a whole mess, which probably isn’t helpful at midnight.\n\nSo it takes me a long time to actually settle down, and most mornings I feel like I slept, but not really rested, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel really keyed up, like before a difficult client conversation or when something in a deal is uncertain, but I wouldn’t say it suddenly peaks in a few minutes like an episode.\n\nSometimes I’ll get myself worked up thinking about what could go wrong, and then I’m off thinking about three other things, like whether I should’ve stayed in the house instead of moving—but no, not like a sudden wave of intense fear, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say that’s probably the part that frustrates me. I can tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent, you don’t need to solve it right now,” and then somehow one little concern turns into another one.\n\nLike I’ll think about one small detail I maybe should have handled differently, and then I’m replaying the whole conversation, and then I’m thinking about something completely separate I need to check on. Before I know it, half an hour has gone by and I’m still stuck on things I meant to just let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that does happen. I’ll try to tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent, you don’t have to solve this right now,” but then my mind doesn’t really listen.\n\nIt’ll start with one small thing, like some little detail I’m not sure I handled perfectly, and then I’m replaying it, and then it reminds me of another thing, and then suddenly I’ve spent half an hour going over all these little pieces I meant to let go. It’s annoying, because it doesn’t feel useful, but it’s hard to stop once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, “on edge” can mean a lot of things, right? In my work I’m moving around and checking things, calls, paperwork, people changing their minds at the last minute—so sometimes it’s hard for me to tell if that’s restlessness or just the way the day goes.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed myself pacing or feeling like I can’t sit still, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I’m busy and then I get caught up in whatever’s in front of me. But I don’t know if I’d call it being on edge exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, more than most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know what “most people” worry about, but it feels like I’ve started worrying about almost everything lately.\n\nWork is a big one—clients, whether a deal will fall through, what the market is doing. And then I’ll be thinking about my family, or money, or whether moving to the condo was actually the right decision. Which is funny because the condo is fine, it’s perfectly fine, and my sister keeps telling me I’m overthinking it, but then I’ll notice some little thing and wonder if I made a mistake.\n\nSo yes, it feels out of proportion sometimes, compared to what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is, at least at work. I used to be pretty quick with listing negotiations or reviewing a contract—well, not careless, but I knew what I was looking for. Lately even straightforward things are taking me much longer.\n\nI’ll sit there checking and rechecking details, and then I think, “Maybe I should look at that clause again,” or “What if I missed something?” And then I end up delaying phone calls I should return because I don’t feel ready yet, like I haven’t checked everything carefully enough.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because real estate already has enough back-and-forth without me adding extra time to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing anything important to you because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve stopped doing anything completely, not like quitting a hobby or avoiding people… but at work, yes, it’s been getting in the way.\n\nLike with listing negotiations or contract reviews, things that used to be pretty routine for me are taking much longer now. I’ll get stuck checking the same details, and then I won’t return a call right away because I feel like I haven’t looked everything over carefully enough yet. And in real estate, if you delay calls, people notice—clients especially. So I haven’t stopped working, but I’m definitely moving slower than I used to because of the worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are affecting your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think it’s caused any big falling-out or anything like that.\n\nMy sister would probably say I’ve been more… repetitive, maybe. I’ll ask her what she thinks about the condo, and then somehow we end up talking about the same thing again a week later. Or I’ll start telling her about some client situation and then remember—oh, she doesn’t really care about escrow timelines, you know?\n\nBut friends and family… I still talk to them. I don’t think I’ve pulled away exactly. It’s more that I wonder if I’m a little tiring lately, but nobody has really said that to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you don't worry as much as others?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really. That’s sort of why I came in—I feel like the worry has spread into everything.\n\nIt’s clients and the market, which I suppose makes sense in my line of work, but then it’s also my family, money, and whether moving to the condo was the right decision. And the condo thing is silly because it’s a perfectly nice place—well, the parking is tighter than I’d like, and the neighbor upstairs walks like he’s wearing boots all day—but still, it’s not some disaster.\n\nIt just feels like my mind treats everything like it’s a major problem, even when nothing that serious is actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or anxiety that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I get anxious, definitely, but it usually feels tied to something—work, a client, the condo, whether I forgot to send something.\n\n“Out of nowhere” is hard for me to say. Like, I’ll be driving and suddenly remember I didn’t follow up with someone, but that’s not really out of nowhere, is it? That’s just… my brain making a list at the worst possible time. So no, I don’t think I’ve had that sudden intense fear for no reason, at least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free? No, I don’t think so. I mean, even things that should be straightforward seem to come with some kind of “what if.”\n\nIt’s like I came in because the worry has gotten into everything—clients, the market, my family, money, the condo. And the condo is a good example because it’s not like anything terrible happened. It’s a nice enough place, but then I’ll find myself wondering, did I make the right decision, should I have waited, what if the market shifts—oh, and then that reminds me of a client who asked me almost the same thing last month.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really point to an area where I feel totally free of it. It feels bigger than what’s actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you can't sit still or have an urge to move around when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I can’t sit still exactly. I mean, I do get up and fuss around sometimes, but then again I’ve always been the sort of person who notices something out of place—like a stack of mail on the counter or a pillow that’s not straight—and then I’m halfway across the room before I even think about it.\n\nBut an urge to move around when I’m not doing something… I’m not sure. If I’m sitting with a client or watching something, I can usually sit. At home I might pop up to check something, but I don’t know whether that’s restlessness or just me being me.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about something specific or do they feel more general and unclear?\n\nPatient: It starts specific, I guess, but then it spreads. Like I’ll worry about a client or the market, and then somehow I’m thinking about money, my family, the condo, whether moving there was the right decision—honestly, things that don’t even need that much attention right then.\n\nSo it’s not one clear thing. It feels like everything has a little hook in it lately, and the amount I’m worrying feels bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are unreasonable, considering the actual circumstances?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I can see that they probably are, at least compared with what’s actually happening. It’s not like there’s one crisis. I came in because I seem to worry about everything now—clients, the market, my family, money, even whether moving to the condo was the right decision.\n\nAnd the condo thing is silly in a way, because it’s fine, it’s a nice place, but I’ll still sit there thinking, “Did I do the right thing?” Then that turns into wondering about interest rates or what a client should do, and then somehow I’m thinking about my family. So yes, it feels out of proportion. Like my mind is treating everything as if it needs urgent attention when it really doesn’t.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’ll get stuck checking the same details, and then I won’t return a call right away because I feel like I haven’t looked everything over carefully enough yet. And in real estate, if you delay calls, people notice—clients especially. So I haven’t stopped working, but I’m definitely moving slower than I used to because of the worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are affecting your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think it’s caused any big falling-out or anything like that.\n\nMy sister would probably say I’ve been more… repetitive, maybe. I’ll ask her what she thinks about the condo, and then somehow we end up talking about the same thing again a week later. Or I’ll start telling her about some client situation and then remember—oh, she doesn’t really care about escrow timelines, you know?\n\nBut friends and family… I still talk to them. I don’t think I’ve pulled away exactly. It’s more that I wonder if I’m a little tiring lately, but nobody has really said that to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you don't worry as much as others?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really. That’s sort of why I came in—I feel like the worry has spread into everything.\n\nIt’s clients and the market, which I suppose makes sense in my line of work, but then it’s also my family, money, and whether moving to the condo was the right decision. And the condo thing is silly because it’s a perfectly nice place—well, the parking is tighter than I’d like, and the neighbor upstairs walks like he’s wearing boots all day—but still, it’s not some disaster.\n\nIt just feels like my mind treats everything like it’s a major problem, even when nothing that serious is actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or anxiety that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I get anxious, definitely, but it usually feels tied to something—work, a client, the condo, whether I forgot to send something.\n\n“Out of nowhere” is hard for me to say. Like, I’ll be driving and suddenly remember I didn’t follow up with someone, but that’s not really out of nowhere, is it? That’s just… my brain making a list at the worst possible time. So no, I don’t think I’ve had that sudden intense fear for no reason, at least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free? No, I don’t think so. I mean, even things that should be straightforward seem to come with some kind of “what if.”\n\nIt’s like I came in because the worry has gotten into everything—clients, the market, my family, money, the condo. And the condo is a good example because it’s not like anything terrible happened. It’s a nice enough place, but then I’ll find myself wondering, did I make the right decision, should I have waited, what if the market shifts—oh, and then that reminds me of a client who asked me almost the same thing last month.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really point to an area where I feel totally free of it. It feels bigger than what’s actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you can't sit still or have an urge to move around when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I can’t sit still exactly. I mean, I do get up and fuss around sometimes, but then again I’ve always been the sort of person who notices something out of place—like a stack of mail on the counter or a pillow that’s not straight—and then I’m halfway across the room before I even think about it.\n\nBut an urge to move around when I’m not doing something… I’m not sure. If I’m sitting with a client or watching something, I can usually sit. At home I might pop up to check something, but I don’t know whether that’s restlessness or just me being me.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about something specific or do they feel more general and unclear?\n\nPatient: It starts specific, I guess, but then it spreads. Like I’ll worry about a client or the market, and then somehow I’m thinking about money, my family, the condo, whether moving there was the right decision—honestly, things that don’t even need that much attention right then.\n\nSo it’s not one clear thing. It feels like everything has a little hook in it lately, and the amount I’m worrying feels bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are unreasonable, considering the actual circumstances?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I can see that they probably are, at least compared with what’s actually happening. It’s not like there’s one crisis. I came in because I seem to worry about everything now—clients, the market, my family, money, even whether moving to the condo was the right decision.\n\nAnd the condo thing is silly in a way, because it’s fine, it’s a nice place, but I’ll still sit there thinking, “Did I do the right thing?” Then that turns into wondering about interest rates or what a client should do, and then somehow I’m thinking about my family. So yes, it feels out of proportion. Like my mind is treating everything as if it needs urgent attention when it really doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually centered around one particular area of your life, like work, or do they seem to cover a wide range of topics?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one area, no. Work is part of it—clients, the market, all of that—but then it sort of jumps tracks. I’ll be thinking about a listing or what’s happening with interest rates, and then suddenly I’m worrying about money, or my family, or whether moving into the condo was even the right decision.\n\nAnd the condo is fine, really, so I don’t know why I keep circling back to it. It’s like everything has become something to worry about, and it feels bigger than what’s actually going on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think it is, though I keep telling myself it’s not that bad. But at work, even things that used to be pretty straightforward—like going through a listing negotiation or reviewing a contract—are taking me much longer than they used to.

I’ll reread the same section and then think, “Wait, did I miss something?” And then I’ll check it again. And honestly, I’ve delayed calling people back sometimes because I don’t feel confident that I’ve checked everything carefully enough yet. Which is embarrassing, because I’ve done this work for years. It’s not like I don’t know how to review a contract. It just feels harder to trust myself lately.

---------- turn 19 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I’m here because I’ve just been worrying so much lately—about almost everything, honestly. My clients, the housing market, my family, money… even silly things like whether moving to the condo was the right decision, and I know lots of people downsize, and it’s probably fine, but I keep turning it over in my head. It just feels bigger than it should, compared to what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’m avoiding things exactly… I mean, I still go to work, I still deal with clients and showings and all that. Sometimes I’ll put off making a call because I’m overthinking how it’ll go, but then in real estate there are always calls you don’t want to make, you know? It’s hard to tell if that’s worry or just… being tired of people changing their minds every five minutes. So, no, I can’t think of a big situation I’ve stopped doing because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or difficulty sleeping?\n\nPatient: Mostly sleep, I think. I do get to bed at a normal time—well, I try to, anyway—but then my mind just starts running. Condo fees, what showings I have the next day, whether I missed some tiny thing in a contract… and then I’m thinking about how one missed detail can turn into a whole mess, which probably isn’t helpful at midnight.\n\nSo it takes me a long time to actually settle down, and most mornings I feel like I slept, but not really rested, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel really keyed up, like before a difficult client conversation or when something in a deal is uncertain, but I wouldn’t say it suddenly peaks in a few minutes like an episode.\n\nSometimes I’ll get myself worked up thinking about what could go wrong, and then I’m off thinking about three other things, like whether I should’ve stayed in the house instead of moving—but no, not like a sudden wave of intense fear, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say that’s probably the part that frustrates me. I can tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent, you don’t need to solve it right now,” and then somehow one little concern turns into another one.\n\nLike I’ll think about one small detail I maybe should have handled differently, and then I’m replaying the whole conversation, and then I’m thinking about something completely separate I need to check on. Before I know it, half an hour has gone by and I’m still stuck on things I meant to just let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that does happen. I’ll try to tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent, you don’t have to solve this right now,” but then my mind doesn’t really listen.\n\nIt’ll start with one small thing, like some little detail I’m not sure I handled perfectly, and then I’m replaying it, and then it reminds me of another thing, and then suddenly I’ve spent half an hour going over all these little pieces I meant to let go. It’s annoying, because it doesn’t feel useful, but it’s hard to stop once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, “on edge” can mean a lot of things, right? In my work I’m moving around and checking things, calls, paperwork, people changing their minds at the last minute—so sometimes it’s hard for me to tell if that’s restlessness or just the way the day goes.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed myself pacing or feeling like I can’t sit still, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I’m busy and then I get caught up in whatever’s in front of me. But I don’t know if I’d call it being on edge exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, more than most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know what “most people” worry about, but it feels like I’ve started worrying about almost everything lately.\n\nWork is a big one—clients, whether a deal will fall through, what the market is doing. And then I’ll be thinking about my family, or money, or whether moving to the condo was actually the right decision. Which is funny because the condo is fine, it’s perfectly fine, and my sister keeps telling me I’m overthinking it, but then I’ll notice some little thing and wonder if I made a mistake.\n\nSo yes, it feels out of proportion sometimes, compared to what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is, at least at work. I used to be pretty quick with listing negotiations or reviewing a contract—well, not careless, but I knew what I was looking for. Lately even straightforward things are taking me much longer.\n\nI’ll sit there checking and rechecking details, and then I think, “Maybe I should look at that clause again,” or “What if I missed something?” And then I end up delaying phone calls I should return because I don’t feel ready yet, like I haven’t checked everything carefully enough.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because real estate already has enough back-and-forth without me adding extra time to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing anything important to you because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve stopped doing anything completely, not like quitting a hobby or avoiding people… but at work, yes, it’s been getting in the way.\n\nLike with listing negotiations or contract reviews, things that used to be pretty routine for me are taking much longer now. I’ll get stuck checking the same details, and then I won’t return a call right away because I feel like I haven’t looked everything over carefully enough yet. And in real estate, if you delay calls, people notice—clients especially. So I haven’t stopped working, but I’m definitely moving slower than I used to because of the worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are affecting your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think it’s caused any big falling-out or anything like that.\n\nMy sister would probably say I’ve been more… repetitive, maybe. I’ll ask her what she thinks about the condo, and then somehow we end up talking about the same thing again a week later. Or I’ll start telling her about some client situation and then remember—oh, she doesn’t really care about escrow timelines, you know?\n\nBut friends and family… I still talk to them. I don’t think I’ve pulled away exactly. It’s more that I wonder if I’m a little tiring lately, but nobody has really said that to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you don't worry as much as others?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really. That’s sort of why I came in—I feel like the worry has spread into everything.\n\nIt’s clients and the market, which I suppose makes sense in my line of work, but then it’s also my family, money, and whether moving to the condo was the right decision. And the condo thing is silly because it’s a perfectly nice place—well, the parking is tighter than I’d like, and the neighbor upstairs walks like he’s wearing boots all day—but still, it’s not some disaster.\n\nIt just feels like my mind treats everything like it’s a major problem, even when nothing that serious is actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or anxiety that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I get anxious, definitely, but it usually feels tied to something—work, a client, the condo, whether I forgot to send something.\n\n“Out of nowhere” is hard for me to say. Like, I’ll be driving and suddenly remember I didn’t follow up with someone, but that’s not really out of nowhere, is it? That’s just… my brain making a list at the worst possible time. So no, I don’t think I’ve had that sudden intense fear for no reason, at least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free? No, I don’t think so. I mean, even things that should be straightforward seem to come with some kind of “what if.”\n\nIt’s like I came in because the worry has gotten into everything—clients, the market, my family, money, the condo. And the condo is a good example because it’s not like anything terrible happened. It’s a nice enough place, but then I’ll find myself wondering, did I make the right decision, should I have waited, what if the market shifts—oh, and then that reminds me of a client who asked me almost the same thing last month.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really point to an area where I feel totally free of it. It feels bigger than what’s actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you can't sit still or have an urge to move around when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I can’t sit still exactly. I mean, I do get up and fuss around sometimes, but then again I’ve always been the sort of person who notices something out of place—like a stack of mail on the counter or a pillow that’s not straight—and then I’m halfway across the room before I even think about it.\n\nBut an urge to move around when I’m not doing something… I’m not sure. If I’m sitting with a client or watching something, I can usually sit. At home I might pop up to check something, but I don’t know whether that’s restlessness or just me being me.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about something specific or do they feel more general and unclear?\n\nPatient: It starts specific, I guess, but then it spreads. Like I’ll worry about a client or the market, and then somehow I’m thinking about money, my family, the condo, whether moving there was the right decision—honestly, things that don’t even need that much attention right then.\n\nSo it’s not one clear thing. It feels like everything has a little hook in it lately, and the amount I’m worrying feels bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are unreasonable, considering the actual circumstances?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I can see that they probably are, at least compared with what’s actually happening. It’s not like there’s one crisis. I came in because I seem to worry about everything now—clients, the market, my family, money, even whether moving to the condo was the right decision.\n\nAnd the condo thing is silly in a way, because it’s fine, it’s a nice place, but I’ll still sit there thinking, “Did I do the right thing?” Then that turns into wondering about interest rates or what a client should do, and then somehow I’m thinking about my family. So yes, it feels out of proportion. Like my mind is treating everything as if it needs urgent attention when it really doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually centered around one particular area of your life, like work, or do they seem to cover a wide range of topics?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one area, no. Work is part of it—clients, the market, all of that—but then it sort of jumps tracks. I’ll be thinking about a listing or what’s happening with interest rates, and then suddenly I’m worrying about money, or my family, or whether moving into the condo was even the right decision.\n\nAnd the condo is fine, really, so I don’t know why I keep circling back to it. It’s like everything has become something to worry about, and it feels bigger than what’s actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is significantly affecting your overall well-being or daily functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is, though I keep telling myself it’s not that bad. But at work, even things that used to be pretty straightforward—like going through a listing negotiation or reviewing a contract—are taking me much longer than they used to.\n\nI’ll reread the same section and then think, “Wait, did I miss something?” And then I’ll check it again. And honestly, I’ve delayed calling people back sometimes because I don’t feel confident that I’ve checked everything carefully enough yet. Which is embarrassing, because I’ve done this work for years. It’s not like I don’t know how to review a contract. It just feels harder to trust myself lately.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’ll get stuck checking the same details, and then I won’t return a call right away because I feel like I haven’t looked everything over carefully enough yet. And in real estate, if you delay calls, people notice—clients especially. So I haven’t stopped working, but I’m definitely moving slower than I used to because of the worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are affecting your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think it’s caused any big falling-out or anything like that.\n\nMy sister would probably say I’ve been more… repetitive, maybe. I’ll ask her what she thinks about the condo, and then somehow we end up talking about the same thing again a week later. I don’t think I’ve pulled away exactly. It’s more that I wonder if I’m a little tiring lately, but nobody has really said that to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you don't worry as much as others?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really. My clients, the housing market, my family, money… even silly things like whether moving to the condo was the right decision, and I know lots of people downsize, and it’s probably fine, but I keep turning it over in my head. It just feels bigger than it should, compared to what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’m avoiding things exactly… I mean, I still go to work, I still deal with clients and showings and all that. Sometimes I’ll put off making a call because I’m overthinking how it’ll go, but then in real estate there are always calls you don’t want to make, you know? It’s hard to tell if that’s worry or just… being tired of people changing their minds every five minutes. So, no, I can’t think of a big situation I’ve stopped doing because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or difficulty sleeping?\n\nPatient: Mostly sleep, I think. I do get to bed at a normal time—well, I try to, anyway—but then my mind just starts running. Condo fees, what showings I have the next day, whether I missed some tiny thing in a contract… and then I’m thinking about how one missed detail can turn into a whole mess, which probably isn’t helpful at midnight.\n\nSo it takes me a long time to actually settle down, and most mornings I feel like I slept, but not really rested, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel really keyed up, like before a difficult client conversation or when something in a deal is uncertain, but I wouldn’t say it suddenly peaks in a few minutes like an episode.\n\nSometimes I’ll get myself worked up thinking about what could go wrong, and then I’m off thinking about three other things, like whether I should’ve stayed in the house instead of moving—but no, not like a sudden wave of intense fear, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say that’s probably the part that frustrates me. I can tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent, you don’t need to solve it right now,” and then somehow one little concern turns into another one.\n\nLike I’ll think about one small detail I maybe should have handled differently, and then I’m replaying the whole conversation, and then I’m thinking about something completely separate I need to check on. Before I know it, half an hour has gone by and I’m still stuck on things I meant to just let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that does happen. I’ll try to tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent, you don’t have to solve this right now,” but then my mind doesn’t really listen.\n\nIt’ll start with one small thing, like some little detail I’m not sure I handled perfectly, and then I’m replaying it, and then it reminds me of another thing, and then suddenly I’ve spent half an hour going over all these little pieces I meant to let go. It’s annoying, because it doesn’t feel useful, but it’s hard to stop once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, “on edge” can mean a lot of things, right? In my work I’m moving around and checking things, calls, paperwork, people changing their minds at the last minute—so sometimes it’s hard for me to tell if that’s restlessness or just the way the day goes.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed myself pacing or feeling like I can’t sit still, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I’m busy and then I get caught up in whatever’s in front of me. But I don’t know if I’d call it being on edge exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, more than most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know what “most people” worry about, but it feels like I’ve started worrying about almost everything lately.\n\nWork is a big one—clients, whether a deal will fall through, what the market is doing. And then I’ll be thinking about my family, or money, or whether moving to the condo was actually the right decision. Which is funny because the condo is fine, it’s perfectly fine, and my sister keeps telling me I’m overthinking it, but then I’ll notice some little thing and wonder if I made a mistake.\n\nSo yes, it feels out of proportion sometimes, compared to what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is, at least at work. I used to be pretty quick with listing negotiations or reviewing a contract—well, not careless, but I knew what I was looking for. Lately even straightforward things are taking me much longer.\n\nI’ll sit there checking and rechecking details, and then I think, “Maybe I should look at that clause again,” or “What if I missed something?” And then I end up delaying phone calls I should return because I don’t feel ready yet, like I haven’t checked everything carefully enough.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because real estate already has enough back-and-forth without me adding extra time to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing anything important to you because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve stopped doing anything completely, not like quitting a hobby or avoiding people… but at work, yes, it’s been getting in the way.\n\nLike with listing negotiations or contract reviews, things that used to be pretty routine for me are taking much longer now. I’ll get stuck checking the same details, and then I won’t return a call right away because I feel like I haven’t looked everything over carefully enough yet. And in real estate, if you delay calls, people notice—clients especially. So I haven’t stopped working, but I’m definitely moving slower than I used to because of the worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are affecting your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think it’s caused any big falling-out or anything like that.\n\nMy sister would probably say I’ve been more… repetitive, maybe. I’ll ask her what she thinks about the condo, and then somehow we end up talking about the same thing again a week later. Or I’ll start telling her about some client situation and then remember—oh, she doesn’t really care about escrow timelines, you know?\n\nBut friends and family… I still talk to them. I don’t think I’ve pulled away exactly. It’s more that I wonder if I’m a little tiring lately, but nobody has really said that to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you don't worry as much as others?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really. That’s sort of why I came in—I feel like the worry has spread into everything.\n\nIt’s clients and the market, which I suppose makes sense in my line of work, but then it’s also my family, money, and whether moving to the condo was the right decision. And the condo thing is silly because it’s a perfectly nice place—well, the parking is tighter than I’d like, and the neighbor upstairs walks like he’s wearing boots all day—but still, it’s not some disaster.\n\nIt just feels like my mind treats everything like it’s a major problem, even when nothing that serious is actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or anxiety that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I get anxious, definitely, but it usually feels tied to something—work, a client, the condo, whether I forgot to send something.\n\n“Out of nowhere” is hard for me to say. Like, I’ll be driving and suddenly remember I didn’t follow up with someone, but that’s not really out of nowhere, is it? That’s just… my brain making a list at the worst possible time. So no, I don’t think I’ve had that sudden intense fear for no reason, at least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free? No, I don’t think so. I mean, even things that should be straightforward seem to come with some kind of “what if.”\n\nIt’s like I came in because the worry has gotten into everything—clients, the market, my family, money, the condo. And the condo is a good example because it’s not like anything terrible happened. It’s a nice enough place, but then I’ll find myself wondering, did I make the right decision, should I have waited, what if the market shifts—oh, and then that reminds me of a client who asked me almost the same thing last month.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really point to an area where I feel totally free of it. It feels bigger than what’s actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you can't sit still or have an urge to move around when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I can’t sit still exactly. I mean, I do get up and fuss around sometimes, but then again I’ve always been the sort of person who notices something out of place—like a stack of mail on the counter or a pillow that’s not straight—and then I’m halfway across the room before I even think about it.\n\nBut an urge to move around when I’m not doing something… I’m not sure. If I’m sitting with a client or watching something, I can usually sit. At home I might pop up to check something, but I don’t know whether that’s restlessness or just me being me.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about something specific or do they feel more general and unclear?\n\nPatient: It starts specific, I guess, but then it spreads. Like I’ll worry about a client or the market, and then somehow I’m thinking about money, my family, the condo, whether moving there was the right decision—honestly, things that don’t even need that much attention right then.\n\nSo it’s not one clear thing. It feels like everything has a little hook in it lately, and the amount I’m worrying feels bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are unreasonable, considering the actual circumstances?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I can see that they probably are, at least compared with what’s actually happening. It’s not like there’s one crisis. I came in because I seem to worry about everything now—clients, the market, my family, money, even whether moving to the condo was the right decision.\n\nAnd the condo thing is silly in a way, because it’s fine, it’s a nice place, but I’ll still sit there thinking, “Did I do the right thing?” Then that turns into wondering about interest rates or what a client should do, and then somehow I’m thinking about my family. So yes, it feels out of proportion. Like my mind is treating everything as if it needs urgent attention when it really doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually centered around one particular area of your life, like work, or do they seem to cover a wide range of topics?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one area, no. Work is part of it—clients, the market, all of that—but then it sort of jumps tracks. I’ll be thinking about a listing or what’s happening with interest rates, and then suddenly I’m worrying about money, or my family, or whether moving into the condo was even the right decision.\n\nAnd the condo is fine, really, so I don’t know why I keep circling back to it. It’s like everything has become something to worry about, and it feels bigger than what’s actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is significantly affecting your overall well-being or daily functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is, though I keep telling myself it’s not that bad. But at work, even things that used to be pretty straightforward—like going through a listing negotiation or reviewing a contract—are taking me much longer than they used to.\n\nI’ll reread the same section and then think, “Wait, did I miss something?” And then I’ll check it again. And honestly, I’ve delayed calling people back sometimes because I don’t feel confident that I’ve checked everything carefully enough yet. Which is embarrassing, because I’ve done this work for years. It’s not like I don’t know how to review a contract. It just feels harder to trust myself lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly it is about what could happen. Like with clients, I’ll start thinking, what if the market shifts, what if I advise someone wrong, what if money becomes a problem later… and then it doesn’t stay there. It wanders into my family, or the condo, and whether moving there was the right decision.\n\nI know some of it is kind of out of proportion, because nothing terrible is actually happening right then. But my mind keeps acting like I need to solve all of it ahead of time."
